Like, “Okay, now I can finally build this supper-club thing, and everyone’s going to see I was meant for something bigger.” I felt excited in this huge way, like unstoppable, like I didn’t need anyone’s approval.\n\nBut at the same time, I was really, really on edge with people. It felt like they were standing in the way of something important, and I couldn’t understand why they didn’t see it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling slowed down and taking a long time to do basic things?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months now, I think. But looking back, yeah, some of what I believed was… pretty detached from where my life actually was.\n\nLike, with the supper-club idea, I honestly believed I was about to build the best bilingual supper club in the city. Not just a good one, not just “maybe this could work someday,” but like it was obvious and inevitable, and anyone with any sense would see it. I remember thinking investors would be stupid not to back me, like they’d be lucky to get in early. It felt like the plan was already basically real because I could see it so clearly. I could picture the menu, the room, the kind of people who’d come, the press, all of it. That was kind of the beginning of the whole thing, I think. After I lost that job, instead of just feeling embarrassed or upset in a normal way, I got really swept up in the supper-club idea. I was acting like it was this huge opportunity, like getting fired had somehow freed me up to do something bigger.\n\nThen after that… later on, it shifted into this slowed-down, heavy feeling. So, yes, the extreme high, all the ideas and certainty about the supper club — that was before the dragging, heavy part I’ve been in more recently.\n\nDoctor: Did the high, energetic period with all the ideas for the supper club stop completely before the slowed-down, heavy feeling started, or did they kind of overlap at first?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I wish I could point to a clear day and say, “That’s when the high part ended and the heavy part began,” but it didn’t feel that neat from the inside.\n\nMy memory of it is more like… the energy and certainty started wearing off, but not all at once. There were days where I still had some of the supper-club stuff in my head — like I’d still think about it or feel like I should be doing something about it — but I didn’t have the same push behind it. It was more messy and gradual, at least from what I can remember. But I also wasn’t exactly tracking it carefully at the time, so I might be putting the pieces together after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve definitely had days where I felt really low, like everything was heavy and I didn’t have much hope about things, but “overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day” — I don’t know if I can honestly measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m crying all day every day, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes it’s more like this dull, flat, weighed-down feeling, and then other times I do get this sense of, “What is the point of any of this?” But I’m not sure if it counts as most of the day or nearly every day. Part of me wants to say they were two different things because they felt so different from each other — the supper-club period was all momentum and meaning, like everything connected, and then later it was more like moving through wet cement.\n\nBut at the same time, they didn’t feel totally unrelated. It wasn’t like one life ended and another one started. It was all tied to the same stretch of time after everything changed, and the supper-club idea was still kind of in the background even when I started feeling slowed down. That’s actually one of the parts that feels the most unsettling, because it’s not just that I’m tired or busy or whatever. It’s like the things that usually make me feel like myself don’t really land anymore.\n\nCooking is a big one. Normally that’s kind of my anchor — even if work is stressful, I’ll mess around with recipes or get excited about making something for people, or I’ll take photos of food and think about the plating or the light. And now it’s just… flat. I can look at ingredients and understand, intellectually, that I used to care about this, but I can’t make myself feel interested in it.\n\nSame with music. I’ll put something on and it’s like it’s happening in another room, even if it’s right there. Pickup soccer too — usually that helps clear my head, and I like the rhythm of it, being around people without having to talk too much. But lately even the idea of going feels like too much, and when I do think about it, there’s no pull.\n\nEven volunteering, which used to make me feel connected to something outside myself, just feels distant. Not bad exactly, just kind of meaningless or muted. It’s like I remember being someone who cared about all these things, but I can’t access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt extremely high and another period where you felt extremely low, with a clear break in between where you felt relatively normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was a clean break like that, no. Not just “in a better mood,” more like I suddenly had this intense excitement, like everything was opening up and I had all these plans, and in my head they all made complete sense.\n\nBut the irritable part was really there too. The sleep and energy part… I mean, what stands out most is that I felt powered by it, like I didn’t want to stop. It didn’t feel like normal energy exactly, more like I was running on this belief that I could push through anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you returned to a normal mood at any point between the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think there was a clear return to normal, no. The whole thing has been going on for about four months now, basically since I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nIt’s not like I can separate it neatly into, “that ended, then I was fine, then another thing started.” It felt more like one long period that kept changing shape. Like the supper-club thing was one part of it, and then later the slowed-down part was another part, but I don’t remember a clean stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high period with the supper-club idea was a distinct episode that stood out from your normal mood and behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely stood out. I didn’t take it as concern—I took it as an attack.\n\nAs far as sleep, I know you asked that too… I don’t want to overstate it, but during that period I definitely felt like I didn’t need as much rest because I was so keyed up and focused on the plans. It went on for several days in a row, not just one random night.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you feel like the high, energetic period with the supper-club idea completely stopped before the slowed-down, heavy feeling began?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about four months, starting after I got fired from the restaurant.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it was a clean stop, honestly. The whole thing feels like it’s been one long stretch since I got fired from the restaurant, which was about four months ago. Like, that’s when things really shifted, and since then it hasn’t felt like I fully went back to my normal baseline.

The high, supper-club part was earlier in that stretch, and then the slowed-down, heavy feeling came in after, but when I try to separate it neatly, it’s hard. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and the energetic version of me was completely gone and then the heavy feeling started on a totally different day. It all feels connected to that firing and everything that happened afterward.

So the best way I can say it is: this whole change has been going on about four months, starting after I got fired. The moods changed during that time, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as one completely stopped before the other began.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… my body feels like it’s not really under my control lately. Like, at first I was moving nonstop — pacing around my apartment all night, going back and forth, making all these notes for this supper-club idea I had, like pages of them, and I couldn’t seem to just sit down or stop moving.\n\nAnd now it’s kind of the opposite, which is honestly scary in a different way. I feel really slowed down. My sister has been noticing it too — she says it takes me forever just to do basic things, like getting my shoes on. I know that sounds small, but it feels like I’m moving through wet cement or something. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel abnormally happy or irritable during the time you were pacing around your apartment and making all those notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” though. It was more like — after I got fired, which normally should’ve made me feel ashamed or scared, I felt this weird rush, like suddenly I was free and I could do anything. Like, “Okay, now I can finally build this supper-club thing, and everyone’s going to see I was meant for something bigger.” I felt excited in this huge way, like unstoppable, like I didn’t need anyone’s approval.\n\nBut at the same time, I was really, really on edge with people. If my sister or anyone questioned it — even just saying, “Maybe slow down,” or “Are you sure this is a good time?” — I’d get furious. Not just annoyed, like I’d take it as them trying to sabotage me or ruin my life. I’d snap and argue and say things I probably shouldn’t have. It felt like they were standing in the way of something important, and I couldn’t understand why they didn’t see it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling slowed down and taking a long time to do basic things?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months now, I think. I mean, I’ve felt really bad, but I don’t know if I’d call it “overwhelmingly sad” in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s more like everything feels heavy and kind of pointless sometimes, but then other times I’m just numb or tired or frustrated with myself because I can’t seem to function normally.\n\nHopeless is a strong word… I guess I’ve had moments where I’ve thought, “Is this just how I am now?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing you’re asking. It’s more this dragged-down feeling that’s been sitting on me, and I can’t really tell where sadness ends and exhaustion starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or beliefs that seem unreal or detached from reality during these episodes of feeling high or low?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it felt unreal at the time, because at the time it felt extremely real — like more real than anything else. I remember thinking investors would be stupid not to back me, like they’d be lucky to get in early. Or at least that’s how it looks when I put the timeline together.\n\nIt’s been going on for about four months altogether, starting after I got fired from the restaurant. That was kind of the beginning of the whole thing, I think. After I lost that job, instead of just feeling embarrassed or upset in a normal way, I got really swept up in the supper-club idea. I was acting like it was this huge opportunity, like getting fired had somehow freed me up to do something bigger.\n\nThen after that… later on, it shifted into this slowed-down, heavy feeling. So, yes, the extreme high, all the ideas and certainty about the supper club — that was before the dragging, heavy part I’ve been in more recently.\n\nDoctor: Did the high, energetic period with all the ideas for the supper club stop completely before the slowed-down, heavy feeling started, or did they kind of overlap at first?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I wish I could point to a clear day and say, “That’s when the high part ended and the heavy part began,” but it didn’t feel that neat from the inside.\n\nMy memory of it is more like… the energy and certainty started wearing off, but not all at once. There were days where I still had some of the supper-club stuff in my head — like I’d still think about it or feel like I should be doing something about it — but I didn’t have the same push behind it. And then the heavy feeling was kind of creeping in around that, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d call it a clean stop. It wasn’t like a switch flipped. It was more messy and gradual, at least from what I can remember. But I also wasn’t exactly tracking it carefully at the time, so I might be putting the pieces together after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve definitely had days where I felt really low, like everything was heavy and I didn’t have much hope about things, but “overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day” — I don’t know if I can honestly measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m crying all day every day, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes it’s more like this dull, flat, weighed-down feeling, and then other times I do get this sense of, “What is the point of any of this?” But I’m not sure if it counts as most of the day or nearly every day. I haven’t really been keeping track, and when I’m in it, the days kind of blur together.\n\nSo… I guess I can say I’ve had periods that felt pretty hopeless and sad, but I’m not sure whether they fit exactly the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you consider the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period as two separate episodes, or did they feel connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate them, honestly. Part of me wants to say they were two different things because they felt so different from each other — the supper-club period was all momentum and meaning, like everything connected, and then later it was more like moving through wet cement.\n\nBut at the same time, they didn’t feel totally unrelated. It wasn’t like one life ended and another one started. It was all tied to the same stretch of time after everything changed, and the supper-club idea was still kind of in the background even when I started feeling slowed down. Not in the same exciting way, more like… I’d think about what I’d been so sure of and feel confused or drained by it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say they felt connected, but not in a way I fully understand. Like chapters of the same bad few months, maybe, rather than completely separate events. But I’m putting that together now looking back — at the time I don’t think I had enough distance to label it one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that feels the most unsettling, because it’s not just that I’m tired or busy or whatever. It’s like the things that usually make me feel like myself don’t really land anymore.\n\nCooking is a big one. Normally that’s kind of my anchor — even if work is stressful, I’ll mess around with recipes or get excited about making something for people, or I’ll take photos of food and think about the plating or the light. Not bad exactly, just kind of meaningless or muted. It’s like I remember being someone who cared about all these things, but I can’t access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt extremely high and another period where you felt extremely low, with a clear break in between where you felt relatively normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was a clean break like that, no. And since then it hasn’t felt like there was a nice normal middle part where I was clearly back to myself and then something separate began. It’s more like the whole four months have been one long, messy thing that changed shape over time.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there was an extremely high period, then a clear normal reset, and then an extremely low period, I don’t think I can honestly say that. It feels connected to the same period after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I would call it sad in a straightforward way. Empty, maybe, but even that word feels… I don’t know, a little too neat.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where most of the day feels kind of hollow or muted, like I’m there but not really in it. Some days I’d probably say I felt blank more than sad. Other days I might not have noticed until later, like, “Oh, I guess I barely felt anything today.” So… maybe? But I don’t want to say yes confidently if I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you didn't need much sleep and had a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a stretch after I got fired where I felt, I don’t know, almost weirdly unstoppable. Not just “in a better mood,” more like I suddenly had this intense excitement, like everything was opening up and I had all these plans, and in my head they all made complete sense.\n\nBut the irritable part was really there too. Like if anyone questioned me or even just suggested I slow down, I’d snap. I’d take it really personally, like they weren’t just disagreeing with me but trying to ruin my life or sabotage the one chance I had to fix everything. I remember feeling like people were being small-minded or trying to hold me back, and I’d get way angrier than the situation probably called for.\n\nSo yes to the extremely high or irritable part for several days. The sleep and energy part… I mean, what stands out most is that I felt powered by it, like I didn’t want to stop. It didn’t feel like normal energy exactly, more like I was running on this belief that I could push through anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you returned to a normal mood at any point between the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think there was a clear return to normal, no. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because from the inside it didn’t feel like, “Okay, that episode ended, and now I’m back to my usual self,” and then later something else started.\n\nThe whole thing feels like one long stretch that began after I got fired from the restaurant. That was about four months ago, and since then it’s just been… this ongoing thing. It changed over time, like different phases of it, but I can’t honestly point to a clean middle period where I felt normal and steady again.\n\nSo between that high period and the slowed-down period, I wouldn’t say I really returned to normal. It all feels connected to the same four months after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were back to your normal self at any point between when you were really into the supper-club idea and when you started feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I kind of already said this, but it doesn’t feel like there was a real “back to normal” point in between. The whole thing has been going on for about four months now, basically since I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nIt’s not like I can separate it neatly into, “that ended, then I was fine, then another thing started.” It felt more like one long period that kept changing shape. Like the supper-club thing was one part of it, and then later the slowed-down part was another part, but I don’t remember a clean stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high period with the supper-club idea was a distinct episode that stood out from your normal mood and behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely stood out. I mean, I guess it’s hard because at the time it didn’t feel wrong to me, it felt like I was finally seeing things clearly, like I had this burst of purpose. But looking back, it was not how I usually am.\n\nAfter I got fired, I felt weirdly unstoppable. Like not just motivated, but almost charged up, excited in this way that felt bigger than me. The supper-club idea felt completely obvious and brilliant to me, like everyone else just needed to catch up.\n\nAnd the biggest thing that makes it stand out is how I reacted when people pushed back. If someone questioned the plan or told me to slow down, I’d just blow up. I’d take it like they were trying to ruin my life, not like they were just concerned or making a suggestion. The heavy, slowed-down part has been there for about three months now, I’d say.\n\nIt’s not like it was just a bad weekend or a couple rough days where I felt off and then bounced back. It’s been this long stretch where most days have that same weight to them. I keep thinking maybe I’m exaggerating because time feels kind of blurry, but when I actually count it out, it’s around three months that it’s been like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you had a decreased need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… after I got fired, that’s when it really started. I felt strangely unstoppable, like I had this energy or excitement that didn’t really match the situation. I wasn’t just “in a better mood,” it was more like I was charged up, like everything I was thinking made total sense and I had to act on it right away.\n\nAnd I was really irritable too. If anyone questioned what I was doing or told me to slow down, I’d just explode at them. It was more messy and gradual, at least from what I can remember. I mean… I’ve definitely had days where I felt really low, like everything was heavy and I didn’t have much hope about things, but “overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day” — I don’t know if I can honestly measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m crying all day every day, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes it’s more like this dull, flat, weighed-down feeling, and then other times I do get this sense of, “What is the point of any of this?” But I’m not sure if it counts as most of the day or nearly every day. I haven’t really been keeping track, and when I’m in it, the days kind of blur together.\n\nSo… I guess I can say I’ve had periods that felt pretty hopeless and sad, but I’m not sure whether they fit exactly the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you consider the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period as two separate episodes, or did they feel connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate them, honestly. Part of me wants to say they were two different things because they felt so different from each other — the supper-club period was all momentum and meaning, like everything connected, and then later it was more like moving through wet cement.\n\nBut at the same time, they didn’t feel totally unrelated. It wasn’t like one life ended and another one started. It was all tied to the same stretch of time after everything changed, and the supper-club idea was still kind of in the background even when I started feeling slowed down. Not in the same exciting way, more like… I’d think about what I’d been so sure of and feel confused or drained by it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say they felt connected, but not in a way I fully understand. Like chapters of the same bad few months, maybe, rather than completely separate events. But I’m putting that together now looking back — at the time I don’t think I had enough distance to label it one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that feels the most unsettling, because it’s not just that I’m tired or busy or whatever. It’s like the things that usually make me feel like myself don’t really land anymore.\n\nCooking is a big one. Normally that’s kind of my anchor — even if work is stressful, I’ll mess around with recipes or get excited about making something for people, or I’ll take photos of food and think about the plating or the light. And now it’s just… flat. I can look at ingredients and understand, intellectually, that I used to care about this, but I can’t make myself feel interested in it.\n\nSame with music. I’ll put something on and it’s like it’s happening in another room, even if it’s right there. Pickup soccer too — usually that helps clear my head, and I like the rhythm of it, being around people without having to talk too much. But lately even the idea of going feels like too much, and when I do think about it, there’s no pull.\n\nEven volunteering, which used to make me feel connected to something outside myself, just feels distant. Not bad exactly, just kind of meaningless or muted. It’s like I remember being someone who cared about all these things, but I can’t access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt extremely high and another period where you felt extremely low, with a clear break in between where you felt relatively normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was a clean break like that, no. I mean, if I’m thinking about the whole stretch, it’s been going on for about four months now, basically starting after I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nThat’s sort of the marker in my head, because before that I wouldn’t say everything was perfect, but this whole… whatever this has been… really started after that happened. And since then it hasn’t felt like there was a nice normal middle part where I was clearly back to myself and then something separate began. It’s more like the whole four months have been one long, messy thing that changed shape over time.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there was an extremely high period, then a clear normal reset, and then an extremely low period, I don’t think I can honestly say that. It feels connected to the same period after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I would call it sad in a straightforward way. Empty, maybe, but even that word feels… I don’t know, a little too neat.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where most of the day feels kind of hollow or muted, like I’m there but not really in it. But if you’re asking specifically whether I can point to a solid two weeks and say, yes, every day or almost every day I felt sad or empty most of the day, I’m not sure I can measure it that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it that way. Some days I’d probably say I felt blank more than sad. Other days I might not have noticed until later, like, “Oh, I guess I barely felt anything today.” So… maybe? But I don’t want to say yes confidently if I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you didn't need much sleep and had a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a stretch after I got fired where I felt, I don’t know, almost weirdly unstoppable. Not just “in a better mood,” more like I suddenly had this intense excitement, like everything was opening up and I had all these plans, and in my head they all made complete sense.\n\nBut the irritable part was really there too. Like if anyone questioned me or even just suggested I slow down, I’d snap. I’d take it really personally, like they weren’t just disagreeing with me but trying to ruin my life or sabotage the one chance I had to fix everything. I remember feeling like people were being small-minded or trying to hold me back, and I’d get way angrier than the situation probably called for.\n\nSo yes to the extremely high or irritable part for several days. The sleep and energy part… I mean, what stands out most is that I felt powered by it, like I didn’t want to stop. It didn’t feel like normal energy exactly, more like I was running on this belief that I could push through anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you returned to a normal mood at any point between the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think there was a clear return to normal, no. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because from the inside it didn’t feel like, “Okay, that episode ended, and now I’m back to my usual self,” and then later something else started.\n\nThe whole thing feels like one long stretch that began after I got fired from the restaurant. That was about four months ago, and since then it’s just been… this ongoing thing. It changed over time, like different phases of it, but I can’t honestly point to a clean middle period where I felt normal and steady again.\n\nSo between that high period and the slowed-down period, I wouldn’t say I really returned to normal. It all feels connected to the same four months after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were back to your normal self at any point between when you were really into the supper-club idea and when you started feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I kind of already said this, but it doesn’t feel like there was a real “back to normal” point in between. The whole thing has been going on for about four months now, basically since I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nIt’s not like I can separate it neatly into, “that ended, then I was fine, then another thing started.” It felt more like one long period that kept changing shape. Like the supper-club thing was one part of it, and then later the slowed-down part was another part, but I don’t remember a clean stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high period with the supper-club idea was a distinct episode that stood out from your normal mood and behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely stood out. I mean, I guess it’s hard because at the time it didn’t feel wrong to me, it felt like I was finally seeing things clearly, like I had this burst of purpose. But looking back, it was not how I usually am.\n\nAfter I got fired, I felt weirdly unstoppable. Like not just motivated, but almost charged up, excited in this way that felt bigger than me. The supper-club idea felt completely obvious and brilliant to me, like everyone else just needed to catch up.\n\nAnd the biggest thing that makes it stand out is how I reacted when people pushed back. If someone questioned the plan or told me to slow down, I’d just blow up. I’d take it like they were trying to ruin my life, not like they were just concerned or making a suggestion. I was so intense about it, and so irritable, like any doubt from someone else felt like an attack.\n\nSo yes, it was different from my normal mood and behavior. It was me, but turned way up in a way that didn’t feel normal afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely more than two weeks. The heavy, slowed-down part has been there for about three months now, I’d say.\n\nIt’s not like it was just a bad weekend or a couple rough days where I felt off and then bounced back. It’s been this long stretch where most days have that same weight to them. I keep thinking maybe I’m exaggerating because time feels kind of blurry, but when I actually count it out, it’s around three months that it’s been like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you had a decreased need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… after I got fired, that’s when it really started. I felt strangely unstoppable, like I had this energy or excitement that didn’t really match the situation. I wasn’t just “in a better mood,” it was more like I was charged up, like everything I was thinking made total sense and I had to act on it right away.\n\nAnd I was really irritable too. If anyone questioned what I was doing or told me to slow down, I’d just explode at them. It felt, in the moment, like they were trying to ruin my life or block me from doing something important, even if they were probably just worried or trying to be reasonable. I didn’t take it as concern—I took it as an attack.\n\nAs far as sleep, I know you asked that too… I don’t want to overstate it, but during that period I definitely felt like I didn’t need as much rest because I was so keyed up and focused on the plans. It went on for several days in a row, not just one random night.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high, energetic period with the supper-club idea completely stopped before the slowed-down, heavy feeling began?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was a clean stop, honestly. I don’t know that I can point to one exact line in the sand, no. The clearest way I can explain it is that this whole thing has been going on for about four months, starting after I got fired from the restaurant.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.0, F31.1, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… my body feels like it’s not really under my control lately. Like, at first I was moving nonstop — pacing around my apartment all night, going back and forth, making all these notes for this supper-club idea I had, like pages of them, and I couldn’t seem to just sit down or stop moving.\n\nAnd now it’s kind of the opposite, which is honestly scary in a different way. I feel really slowed down. My sister has been noticing it too — she says it takes me forever just to do basic things, like getting my shoes on. I know that sounds small, but it feels like I’m moving through wet cement or something. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel abnormally happy or irritable during the time you were pacing around your apartment and making all those notes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” though. It was more like — after I got fired, which normally should’ve made me feel ashamed or scared, I felt this weird rush, like suddenly I was free and I could do anything. Like, “Okay, now I can finally build this supper-club thing, and everyone’s going to see I was meant for something bigger.” I felt excited in this huge way, like unstoppable, like I didn’t need anyone’s approval.\n\nBut at the same time, I was really, really on edge with people. If my sister or anyone questioned it — even just saying, “Maybe slow down,” or “Are you sure this is a good time?” — I’d get furious. Not just annoyed, like I’d take it as them trying to sabotage me or ruin my life. I’d snap and argue and say things I probably shouldn’t have. It felt like they were standing in the way of something important, and I couldn’t understand why they didn’t see it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling slowed down and taking a long time to do basic things?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months now, I think. The heavy, slowed-down part. It didn’t feel like a single day where it suddenly switched on, but looking back, yeah, around three months I’ve been like this — taking forever with basic things, moving slowly, feeling weighed down in my body. It’s long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or me being tired anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods where you felt overwhelmingly sad or hopeless during the past three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I’ve felt really bad, but I don’t know if I’d call it “overwhelmingly sad” in a clean way, if that makes sense. It’s more like everything feels heavy and kind of pointless sometimes, but then other times I’m just numb or tired or frustrated with myself because I can’t seem to function normally.\n\nHopeless is a strong word… I guess I’ve had moments where I’ve thought, “Is this just how I am now?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing you’re asking. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a neat answer for it. It hasn’t been like I’m crying all day every day or anything. It’s more this dragged-down feeling that’s been sitting on me, and I can’t really tell where sadness ends and exhaustion starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or beliefs that seem unreal or detached from reality during these episodes of feeling high or low?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it felt unreal at the time, because at the time it felt extremely real — like more real than anything else. But looking back, yeah, some of what I believed was… pretty detached from where my life actually was.\n\nLike, with the supper-club idea, I honestly believed I was about to build the best bilingual supper club in the city. Not just a good one, not just “maybe this could work someday,” but like it was obvious and inevitable, and anyone with any sense would see it. I remember thinking investors would be stupid not to back me, like they’d be lucky to get in early. And the weird part is, I didn’t have a job then, I didn’t have the money lined up, I didn’t really have the practical pieces in place the way I was acting like I did.\n\nBut in my head, that didn’t matter. It felt like the plan was already basically real because I could see it so clearly. I could picture the menu, the room, the kind of people who’d come, the press, all of it. So if someone questioned it, I didn’t think, “Oh, maybe I’m getting ahead of myself.” I thought they just didn’t understand how big it was going to be. So… yeah, I guess that belief was pretty far from reality, even if it didn’t feel that way while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: Did the periods where you felt extremely high and had all those ideas for the supper club happen before the periods where you felt slowed down and heavy?\n\nPatient: Yeah — the high period came first. Or at least that’s how it looks when I put the timeline together.\n\nIt’s been going on for about four months altogether, starting after I got fired from the restaurant. That was kind of the beginning of the whole thing, I think. After I lost that job, instead of just feeling embarrassed or upset in a normal way, I got really swept up in the supper-club idea. I was acting like it was this huge opportunity, like getting fired had somehow freed me up to do something bigger.\n\nThen after that… later on, it shifted into this slowed-down, heavy feeling. So, yes, the extreme high, all the ideas and certainty about the supper club — that was before the dragging, heavy part I’ve been in more recently.\n\nDoctor: Did the high, energetic period with all the ideas for the supper club stop completely before the slowed-down, heavy feeling started, or did they kind of overlap at first?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I wish I could point to a clear day and say, “That’s when the high part ended and the heavy part began,” but it didn’t feel that neat from the inside.\n\nMy memory of it is more like… the energy and certainty started wearing off, but not all at once. There were days where I still had some of the supper-club stuff in my head — like I’d still think about it or feel like I should be doing something about it — but I didn’t have the same push behind it. And then the heavy feeling was kind of creeping in around that, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d call it a clean stop. It wasn’t like a switch flipped. It was more messy and gradual, at least from what I can remember. But I also wasn’t exactly tracking it carefully at the time, so I might be putting the pieces together after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve definitely had days where I felt really low, like everything was heavy and I didn’t have much hope about things, but “overwhelmingly sad or hopeless for most of the day, nearly every day” — I don’t know if I can honestly measure it that precisely.\n\nIt hasn’t been like I’m crying all day every day, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes it’s more like this dull, flat, weighed-down feeling, and then other times I do get this sense of, “What is the point of any of this?” But I’m not sure if it counts as most of the day or nearly every day. I haven’t really been keeping track, and when I’m in it, the days kind of blur together.\n\nSo… I guess I can say I’ve had periods that felt pretty hopeless and sad, but I’m not sure whether they fit exactly the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did you consider the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period as two separate episodes, or did they feel connected in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to separate them, honestly. Part of me wants to say they were two different things because they felt so different from each other — the supper-club period was all momentum and meaning, like everything connected, and then later it was more like moving through wet cement.\n\nBut at the same time, they didn’t feel totally unrelated. It wasn’t like one life ended and another one started. It was all tied to the same stretch of time after everything changed, and the supper-club idea was still kind of in the background even when I started feeling slowed down. Not in the same exciting way, more like… I’d think about what I’d been so sure of and feel confused or drained by it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say they felt connected, but not in a way I fully understand. Like chapters of the same bad few months, maybe, rather than completely separate events. But I’m putting that together now looking back — at the time I don’t think I had enough distance to label it one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy during this slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that feels the most unsettling, because it’s not just that I’m tired or busy or whatever. It’s like the things that usually make me feel like myself don’t really land anymore.\n\nCooking is a big one. Normally that’s kind of my anchor — even if work is stressful, I’ll mess around with recipes or get excited about making something for people, or I’ll take photos of food and think about the plating or the light. And now it’s just… flat. I can look at ingredients and understand, intellectually, that I used to care about this, but I can’t make myself feel interested in it.\n\nSame with music. I’ll put something on and it’s like it’s happening in another room, even if it’s right there. Pickup soccer too — usually that helps clear my head, and I like the rhythm of it, being around people without having to talk too much. But lately even the idea of going feels like too much, and when I do think about it, there’s no pull.\n\nEven volunteering, which used to make me feel connected to something outside myself, just feels distant. Not bad exactly, just kind of meaningless or muted. It’s like I remember being someone who cared about all these things, but I can’t access that feeling right now.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt extremely high and another period where you felt extremely low, with a clear break in between where you felt relatively normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was a clean break like that, no. I mean, if I’m thinking about the whole stretch, it’s been going on for about four months now, basically starting after I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nThat’s sort of the marker in my head, because before that I wouldn’t say everything was perfect, but this whole… whatever this has been… really started after that happened. And since then it hasn’t felt like there was a nice normal middle part where I was clearly back to myself and then something separate began. It’s more like the whole four months have been one long, messy thing that changed shape over time.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there was an extremely high period, then a clear normal reset, and then an extremely low period, I don’t think I can honestly say that. It feels connected to the same period after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I would call it sad in a straightforward way. Empty, maybe, but even that word feels… I don’t know, a little too neat.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where most of the day feels kind of hollow or muted, like I’m there but not really in it. But if you’re asking specifically whether I can point to a solid two weeks and say, yes, every day or almost every day I felt sad or empty most of the day, I’m not sure I can measure it that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it that way. Some days I’d probably say I felt blank more than sad. Other days I might not have noticed until later, like, “Oh, I guess I barely felt anything today.” So… maybe? But I don’t want to say yes confidently if I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you didn't need much sleep and had a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a stretch after I got fired where I felt, I don’t know, almost weirdly unstoppable. Not just “in a better mood,” more like I suddenly had this intense excitement, like everything was opening up and I had all these plans, and in my head they all made complete sense.\n\nBut the irritable part was really there too. I remember feeling like people were being small-minded or trying to hold me back, and I’d get way angrier than the situation probably called for.\n\nSo yes to the extremely high or irritable part for several days. The sleep and energy part… I mean, what stands out most is that I felt powered by it, like I didn’t want to stop. It didn’t feel like normal energy exactly, more like I was running on this belief that I could push through anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you returned to a normal mood at any point between the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think there was a clear return to normal, no. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because from the inside it didn’t feel like, “Okay, that episode ended, and now I’m back to my usual self,” and then later something else started.\n\nThe whole thing feels like one long stretch that began after I got fired from the restaurant. That was about four months ago, and since then it’s just been… this ongoing thing. It changed over time, like different phases of it, but I can’t honestly point to a clean middle period where I felt normal and steady again.\n\nSo between that high period and the slowed-down period, I wouldn’t say I really returned to normal. It all feels connected to the same four months after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were back to your normal self at any point between when you were really into the supper-club idea and when you started feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. The whole thing has been going on for about four months now, basically since I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nIt’s not like I can separate it neatly into, “that ended, then I was fine, then another thing started.” It felt more like one long period that kept changing shape. Like the supper-club thing was one part of it, and then later the slowed-down part was another part, but I don’t remember a clean stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high period with the supper-club idea was a distinct episode that stood out from your normal mood and behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely stood out. It feels connected to the same period after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I would call it sad in a straightforward way. Empty, maybe, but even that word feels… I don’t know, a little too neat.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where most of the day feels kind of hollow or muted, like I’m there but not really in it. But if you’re asking specifically whether I can point to a solid two weeks and say, yes, every day or almost every day I felt sad or empty most of the day, I’m not sure I can measure it that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it that way. Some days I’d probably say I felt blank more than sad. Other days I might not have noticed until later, like, “Oh, I guess I barely felt anything today.” So… maybe? But I don’t want to say yes confidently if I’m not sure what counts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you didn't need much sleep and had a lot of energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a stretch after I got fired where I felt, I don’t know, almost weirdly unstoppable. Not just “in a better mood,” more like I suddenly had this intense excitement, like everything was opening up and I had all these plans, and in my head they all made complete sense.\n\nBut the irritable part was really there too. Like if anyone questioned me or even just suggested I slow down, I’d snap. I’d take it really personally, like they weren’t just disagreeing with me but trying to ruin my life or sabotage the one chance I had to fix everything. I remember feeling like people were being small-minded or trying to hold me back, and I’d get way angrier than the situation probably called for.\n\nSo yes to the extremely high or irritable part for several days. The sleep and energy part… I mean, what stands out most is that I felt powered by it, like I didn’t want to stop. It didn’t feel like normal energy exactly, more like I was running on this belief that I could push through anything.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you returned to a normal mood at any point between the high period with the supper-club idea and the later slowed-down period?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think there was a clear return to normal, no. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because from the inside it didn’t feel like, “Okay, that episode ended, and now I’m back to my usual self,” and then later something else started.\n\nThe whole thing feels like one long stretch that began after I got fired from the restaurant. That was about four months ago, and since then it’s just been… this ongoing thing. It changed over time, like different phases of it, but I can’t honestly point to a clean middle period where I felt normal and steady again.\n\nSo between that high period and the slowed-down period, I wouldn’t say I really returned to normal. It all feels connected to the same four months after losing the job.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were back to your normal self at any point between when you were really into the supper-club idea and when you started feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I kind of already said this, but it doesn’t feel like there was a real “back to normal” point in between. The whole thing has been going on for about four months now, basically since I got fired from the restaurant.\n\nIt’s not like I can separate it neatly into, “that ended, then I was fine, then another thing started.” It felt more like one long period that kept changing shape. Like the supper-club thing was one part of it, and then later the slowed-down part was another part, but I don’t remember a clean stretch where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high period with the supper-club idea was a distinct episode that stood out from your normal mood and behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely stood out. I mean, I guess it’s hard because at the time it didn’t feel wrong to me, it felt like I was finally seeing things clearly, like I had this burst of purpose. But looking back, it was not how I usually am.\n\nAfter I got fired, I felt weirdly unstoppable. Like not just motivated, but almost charged up, excited in this way that felt bigger than me. The supper-club idea felt completely obvious and brilliant to me, like everyone else just needed to catch up.\n\nAnd the biggest thing that makes it stand out is how I reacted when people pushed back. If someone questioned the plan or told me to slow down, I’d just blow up. I’d take it like they were trying to ruin my life, not like they were just concerned or making a suggestion. I was so intense about it, and so irritable, like any doubt from someone else felt like an attack.\n\nSo yes, it was different from my normal mood and behavior. It was me, but turned way up in a way that didn’t feel normal afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely more than two weeks. The heavy, slowed-down part has been there for about three months now, I’d say.\n\nIt’s not like it was just a bad weekend or a couple rough days where I felt off and then bounced back. It’s been this long stretch where most days have that same weight to them. I keep thinking maybe I’m exaggerating because time feels kind of blurry, but when I actually count it out, it’s around three months that it’s been like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely high or irritable for several days in a row, where you had a decreased need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… after I got fired, that’s when it really started. I felt strangely unstoppable, like I had this energy or excitement that didn’t really match the situation. I wasn’t just “in a better mood,” it was more like I was charged up, like everything I was thinking made total sense and I had to act on it right away.\n\nAnd I was really irritable too. If anyone questioned what I was doing or told me to slow down, I’d just explode at them. It felt, in the moment, like they were trying to ruin my life or block me from doing something important, even if they were probably just worried or trying to be reasonable. I didn’t take it as concern—I took it as an attack.\n\nAs far as sleep, I know you asked that too… I don’t want to overstate it, but during that period I definitely felt like I didn’t need as much rest because I was so keyed up and focused on the plans. It went on for several days in a row, not just one random night.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the high, energetic period with the supper-club idea completely stopped before the slowed-down, heavy feeling began?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was a clean stop, honestly. Like, that’s when things really shifted, and since then it hasn’t felt like I fully went back to my normal baseline.\n\nThe high, supper-club part was earlier in that stretch, and then the slowed-down, heavy feeling came in after, but when I try to separate it neatly, it’s hard. It wasn’t like I woke up one morning and the energetic version of me was completely gone and then the heavy feeling started on a totally different day. It all feels connected to that firing and everything that happened afterward.\n\nSo the best way I can say it is: this whole change has been going on about four months, starting after I got fired. The moods changed during that time, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as one completely stopped before the other began.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was a distinct point where the high, energetic feelings stopped and the slowed-down, heavy feelings began?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one exact line in the sand, no. It all feels tied to that same stretch after I lost the job."
